  • Treating India‑Turkey defence cooperation as strategic alignment; the Bayraktar TB2 procurement was a limited third‑party contract and the 2024 JCM only agreed to explore non‑sensitive cooperation without a government‑to‑government defence framework.
  • Equating India‑Egypt food‑security wheat transfers with a formal agricultural trade agreement; the 2023 wheat supply was a government‑to‑government humanitarian‑commercial arrangement, not a binding FTA clause.
  • Assuming the India‑Thailand Trilateral Highway is fully operational; the Myanmar segment remains incomplete due to post‑coup security challenges, and the 2027 target is aspirational.

India‑Italy Strategic Partnership: Defence, Space, and Technology Cooperation

The India‑Italy Strategic Partnership was elevated in March 2023 with the adoption of a Joint Strategic Vision that identified defence industrial cooperation, space exploration, and critical technology as the three pillars of engagement. The 2023 Defence Cooperation Agreement, signed during the Italian Defence Minister’s visit to New Delhi, established a Joint Working Group (JWG) on Defence Industry that meets biannually to coordinate co‑development of naval platforms, including a feasibility study for a jointly designed offshore patrol vessel. In June 2024 the two navies conducted the first edition of Exercise “Indra‑Med”, a maritime drill in the Arabian Sea focused on anti‑submarine warfare and information‑sharing protocols.

Space cooperation is anchored in the 2022 Memorandum of Understanding between the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) and the Italian Space Agency (ASI). The MoU enabled the launch of the Italian‑built “PRISMA‑2” hyperspectral satellite on a PSLV‑C55 mission in April 2024, and paved the way for joint development of a synthetic aperture radar (SAR) payload for the upcoming NISAR follow‑on mission. A 2024 Joint Committee on Space met in Bengaluru to finalise data‑exchange standards and to explore cooperation on lunar surface experiments under the Artemis Accords framework, which Italy signed in 2023.

On the technology front, the 2023 India‑Italy Digital Partnership launched a Cyber Security Dialogue that produced a 2024 framework for sharing threat‑intelligence on critical infrastructure. The two sides also agreed to set up a Joint Centre of Excellence for Artificial Intelligence in Pune, co‑funded by the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology and the Italian Ministry of Economic Development. Bilateral trade crossed €12 billion in FY 2023‑24, with machinery, pharmaceuticals, and automotive components dominating the basket. The 2024 India‑Italy Business Forum in Milan identified 20 priority projects worth €3.2 billion, including a green‑hydrogen hub in Gujarat backed by Italian engineering firm Ansaldo Energia.

India‑Philippines Defence Cooperation: BrahMos Export, Maritime Domain Awareness, and Joint Exercises

The defence relationship between India and the Philippines entered a new phase with the January 2022 contract for the supply of three BrahMos shore‑based anti‑ship missile batteries, valued at US$375 million. The first battery was delivered in March 2024 and declared operational after a live‑fire test off the coast of Luzon in June 2024. The 2023 Defence Cooperation Agreement, signed during the Philippine Defence Secretary’s visit to New Delhi, institutionalised a Defence Industry Joint Working Group and a Maritime Security Dialogue that convenes annually.

Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) is the centrepiece of the 2024 implementation plan. India gifted a Coastal Surveillance Radar (CSR) system to the Philippine Coast Guard in August 2024, integrated with the Indian Navy’s Information Fusion Centre – Indian Ocean Region (IFC‑IOR) for real‑time white‑shipping data exchange. The two navies conducted the inaugural “Samudra‑Lakas” bilateral exercise in the South China Sea in October 2024, focusing on visit‑board‑search‑seizure (VBSS) drills, joint anti‑piracy patrols, and interoperability of communication protocols.

Capacity building remains a priority. The 2024‑25 training calendar includes 12 Philippine Navy officers attending the Indian Naval Academy’s advanced navigation course, and a Philippine Air Force delegation participating in the “Garuda‑Shakti” air‑force exercise in Jodhpur. The Philippines has expressed interest in co‑producing the Light Combat Helicopter (LCH) and the Akash missile system; a feasibility study was launched in November 2024 under the Defence Industry JWG. Trade in defence‑related goods reached US$120 million in FY 2023‑24, and the 2024 India‑Philippines Joint Commission on Economic Cooperation set a target of US$500 million by 2028, with emphasis on shipbuilding and cyber‑security services.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “The BrahMos missile sale to the Philippines automatically upgrades the bilateral relationship to a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership.” Identify the flaw in this statement.
The flaw is the assumption that a single high‑value platform transfer equates to the highest diplomatic tier. A Comprehensive Strategic Partnership requires a constellation of institutional mechanisms — a 2+2 ministerial dialogue, a reciprocal logistics support agreement, a co‑development roadmap, and regular strategic dialogues across multiple domains. As of 2024, India‑Philippines ties have a Defence Cooperation Agreement and a Maritime Security Dialogue, but lack a 2+2 dialogue, a logistics pact, and a formal co‑development framework. Therefore, the relationship remains a Strategic Partnership, not a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership.
Exam‑day strategy
When evaluating the depth of a bilateral defence relationship, map the institutional architecture first: 2+2 dialogue, logistics agreement, joint working groups, and co‑development projects. A single platform sale is a necessary but not sufficient indicator of strategic升级.

India‑Malaysia Comprehensive Strategic Partnership: Defence, Digital Economy, and Connectivity

The India‑Malaysia Comprehensive Strategic Partnership, upgraded in September 2023 during the Malaysian Prime Minister’s state visit, rests on three pillars: defence and security, digital economy, and multimodal connectivity. The 2023 Defence Cooperation Memorandum established a Defence Industry Joint Committee that met in Kuala Lumpur in February 2024 to explore co‑production of the Indian‑designed Advanced Light Helicopter (ALH) for the Royal Malaysian Air Force and joint maintenance of the Sukhoi‑30MKM fleet.

Maritime security cooperation is operationalised through the biennial “Harimau‑Shakti” naval exercise, with the 2024 edition held in the Strait of Malacca focusing on anti‑piracy, search‑and‑rescue, and information‑fusion using the Indian Navy’s IFC‑IOR and Malaysia’s Maritime Domain Awareness System. A White Shipping Agreement signed in March 2024 enables automatic exchange of merchant‑vessel tracking data, complementing the 2022 Mutual Logistics Support Agreement that permits reciprocal refuelling and replenishment at each other’s naval bases.

On the digital front, the 2023 India‑Malaysia Digital Economy MoU launched a FinTech Innovation Corridor linking Bengaluru’s startup ecosystem with Kuala Lumpur’s Islamic finance hub. The 2024 pilot of UPI‑DuitNow interoperability allows real‑time retail payments for the 150,000‑strong Indian diaspora and Malaysian tourists. A Joint Working Group on Cyber Security, constituted in July 2024, finalised a framework for critical‑infrastructure threat intelligence sharing and joint incident‑response exercises.

Connectivity projects include the proposed Chennai‑Port Klang direct shipping service, inaugurated in January 2024, reducing transit time by 30 percent. The 2024 India‑Malaysia Joint Commission on Trade identified 15 priority sectors — electronics, pharmaceuticals, halal food processing, and renewable energy — with a target of US$25 billion bilateral trade by 2028. Malaysia’s sovereign wealth fund Khazanah Nasional committed US$500 million to India’s National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF) for green‑energy projects, signalling deepening financial integration.

India‑Spain Strategic Partnership: Renewable Energy, Defence Industrial Cooperation, and Cultural Diplomacy

The India‑Spain Strategic Partnership, formalised in April 2023 with a Joint Declaration, prioritises renewable energy, defence industrial collaboration, and people‑to‑people ties. The 2023 Renewable Energy Cooperation Agreement set a joint target of 10 GW of solar and wind capacity by 2030, leveraging Spanish firms such as Iberdrola and Acciona for project development in Rajasthan and Gujarat. The first 500 MW solar park, co‑developed by NTPC and Iberdrola, achieved financial closure in November 2024.

Defence industrial cooperation advanced with the 2024 signing of a Defence Technology and Trade Initiative (DTTI)‑style agreement establishing a Joint Working Group on Defence Production. The JWG identified co‑development of a medium‑altitude long‑endurance (MALE) unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) and joint production of the C‑295 transport aircraft components in India under the “Make in India” programme. The Spanish Navy and Indian Navy conducted the inaugural “Varuna‑Indra” bilateral exercise in the Mediterranean in June 2024, focusing on anti‑submarine warfare and maritime interdiction.

Cultural diplomacy is anchored in the 2023 Cultural Exchange Programme, which facilitated the “India‑Spain Year of Culture” in 2024, featuring over 200 events across 30 Spanish cities. The 2024 agreement on mutual recognition of academic qualifications aims to boost student mobility; currently 8,000 Indian students are enrolled in Spanish universities, primarily in engineering and business. The 2024 India‑Spain Business Summit in Madrid announced 12 joint ventures worth €1.8 billion in sectors ranging from high‑speed rail signalling to water‑management technology.

  • Assuming a single missile export (e.g., BrahMos to the Philippines) automatically confers the highest strategic partnership tier; the full suite of institutional mechanisms must be present.
  • Confusing a White Shipping Agreement with a reciprocal logistics support agreement; the former enables merchant‑vessel data exchange, the latter provides military refuelling, repair, and replenishment rights.
  • Overstating the operational readiness of co‑development projects such as the India‑Malaysia ALH or India‑Spain MALE UAV; most remain at feasibility or prototype stage as of 2024.
  • Treating a cultural exchange programme as a substitute for hard security alignment; soft‑power initiatives complement but do not replace defence dialogues.
  • Neglecting the connectivity bottleneck in India‑Malaysia relations; the Strait of Malacca remains a chokepoint where both nations depend on third‑party shipping lanes.
  • Equating a renewable‑energy MoU with a binding investment treaty; financial closure and regulatory approvals are separate, often protracted processes.
  • Ignoring the role of sovereign wealth funds (e.g., Khazanah Nasional) as strategic vectors; their investment decisions can shape bilateral leverage beyond government‑to‑government agreements.

The advanced bilateral frameworks outlined above illustrate how institutional depth, multi‑domain engagement, and measurable deliverables differentiate strategic partnerships from transactional ties.

India‑Italy Strategic Partnership: Defence Industrial Cooperation, Space Situational Awareness, and Clean Energy

The India‑Italy Strategic Partnership was elevated during Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s state visit to Rome in March 2023, when both sides signed a Joint Statement that identified three priority pillars: defence industrial cooperation, space situational awareness (SSA), and clean energy transition. A Defence Cooperation Agreement, originally concluded in 2017, was operationalised through a Joint Working Group (JWG) on Defence Industrial Cooperation that met for the first time in New Delhi in September 2023. The JWG mandated co‑development of naval platforms, including the integration of Italian Leonardo‑supplied electronic warfare suites onto Indian Navy frigates, and explored joint production of the AW159 Wildcat helicopter for the Indian Coast Guard.

In the space domain, the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) and the Italian Space Agency (ASI) signed a Memorandum of Understanding in June 2023 to share SSA data from the Italian Space Surveillance and Tracking (SST) network and India’s NETRA (Network for Space Object Tracking and Analysis) radars. The agreement provides for real‑time conjunction alerts, joint calibration campaigns, and a coordinated debris‑mitigation roadmap aligned with the UN COPUOS guidelines. A joint ISRO‑ASI working group convened in Bengaluru in January 2024 to finalise data‑exchange protocols and to plan a combined satellite‑laser‑ranging experiment using the Italian Matera Laser Ranging Observatory.

Clean energy cooperation leverages Italy’s membership in the International Solar Alliance (ISA) since 2021 and its advanced green‑hydrogen ecosystem. In November 2022, Enel Green Power and NTPC Limited signed a MoU to develop a 1 GW green‑hydrogen hub in Gujarat, targeting production of 200,000 tonnes per annum by 2030. The 2023 Joint Statement committed both governments to mobilise €1.5 billion in blended finance for Indian renewable projects, with the European Investment Bank (EIB) acting as a co‑financier. A dedicated India‑Italy Clean Energy Working Group, chaired by the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy and Italy’s Ministry of Ecological Transition, held its inaugural meeting in Milan in April 2024 to monitor project pipelines and regulatory harmonisation.

Institutional depth is reinforced by a High‑Level Strategic Dialogue (HLSD) at Foreign Minister level, a Defence Policy Group (DPG) at Secretary level, and a Space Cooperation Committee (SCC) at agency level. These mechanisms ensure that political guidance translates into time‑bound deliverables, distinguishing the partnership from transactional engagements.

India‑Thailand Connectivity and Security Cooperation: Trilateral Highway, Joint Military Exercises, and Mekong Sub‑Regional Engagement

Connectivity between India and Thailand is anchored in the India‑Myanmar‑Thailand Trilateral Highway (IMTTH), a 1,360 km corridor linking Moreh (Manipur) to Mae Sot (Tak) via Myanmar’s Kalewa‑Yargyi segment. The project, conceived in 2012 under the ASEAN‑India Connectivity Master Plan, saw its first Joint Technical Committee meeting in Nay Pyi Taw in July 2022, where both sides agreed to accelerate the Kalewa‑Yargyi road upgrade to double‑lane standards by 2026. India extended a $100 million line of credit in 2023 for the Myanmar portion, while Thailand committed to financing the Mae Sot‑Myawaddy bridge reconstruction, slated for completion in 2025.

Security cooperation is institutionalised through the Annual Defence Dialogue (ADD) at Defence Secretary level, last held in Bangkok in February 2024, and a series of bilateral exercises. The army‑level “Maitree” exercise, conducted alternately in India and Thailand since 2006, was held in Himachal Pradesh in October 2023 with a focus on counter‑insurgency and jungle warfare. The naval “Indo‑Thai CORPAT” (Coordinated Patrol) has been conducted biannually since 2018; the 2024 edition in the Andaman Sea involved Indian Navy’s INS Kiltan and Royal Thai Navy’s HTMS Kraburi, practising visit‑board‑search‑seizure (VBSS) drills and maritime domain awareness (MDA) data exchange via the White Shipping Agreement signed in 2021.

Sub‑regional engagement runs through the Mekong‑Ganga Cooperation (MGC) framework, established in 2000. Thailand, as the current MGC chair (2023‑2025), has prioritised the “MGC Digital Connectivity Corridor” linking India’s North‑East with the Mekong economies through fibre‑optic links and a proposed cross‑border e‑commerce platform. The 2023 MGC Ministerial Meeting in Vientiane endorsed a $50 million Indian grant for capacity‑building in digital skills and public‑health surveillance across the five Mekong countries.

Economic integration is advancing via the Early Harvest Scheme (EHS) under the ASEAN‑India Free Trade Area, which reduced tariffs on 4,000 product lines in 2022. Bilateral trade reached $15.2 billion in FY 2023‑24, with Indian pharmaceuticals, automotive components, and Thai processed food dominating the basket. A Joint Trade Committee (JTC) meeting in New Delhi in June 2024 identified non‑tariff barriers in sanitary‑phytosanitary certification and agreed to mutual recognition of organic standards by 2025.

Technique: Comparative Mechanism Mapping
Compare the institutional frameworks that govern India’s defence cooperation with Italy and the Philippines. Highlight one structural similarity and one key difference.
Similarity: Both partnerships operate a Joint Working Group (JWG) on Defence Industrial Cooperation that meets annually and reports to a higher‑level Defence Policy Dialogue (Italy) or Joint Defence Cooperation Committee (Philippines). Difference: The Italy JWG is embedded within a broader High‑Level Strategic Dialogue at Foreign Minister level, giving it direct political oversight, whereas the Philippines mechanism remains at the Defence Secretary level with no dedicated ministerial‑track review, limiting strategic direction during leadership transitions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “institutional depth” in a bilateral partnership, list the hierarchy of dialogues (Ministerial → Secretary → Working Group) and note whether each tier has a fixed periodicity. This pattern appears repeatedly for Italy, UK, Germany, and Thailand; memorising the tier names and meeting frequencies yields quick, high‑scoring answers.

India‑Philippines Defence Cooperation: BrahMos Missile Export, Maritime Domain Awareness, and South China Sea Coordination

The landmark defence contract between India and the Philippines was signed on 28 January 2022, when the Philippine Department of National Defence (DND) procured three batteries of the BrahMos shore‑based anti‑ship missile system for $374.9 million. The deal, executed under a Government‑to‑Government (G2G) framework, marked the first export of the supersonic cruise missile co‑developed by India’s DRDO and Russia’s NPO Mashinostroyenia. Deliveries commenced in March 2024, with the first battery declared operational at the Philippine Navy’s Fort San Antonio base in June 2024. A Joint Project Monitoring Committee (JPMC), co‑chaired by DRDO and the Philippine DND, oversees integration, training, and lifecycle support.

Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) cooperation was formalised through a White Shipping Agreement (WSA) signed in Manila on 12 September 2023. The WSA enables automatic exchange of Automatic Identification System (AIS) data between the Indian Navy’s Information Fusion Centre – Indian Ocean Region (IFC‑IOR) and the Philippine Coast Guard’s National Coast Watch Center. The first data‑feed test in December 2023 achieved 98 % message completeness for vessels transiting the South China Sea (SCS) and the Philippine Sea. A bilateral Coast Guard exercise, “Sahayog‑2023”, conducted off Palawan in November 2023, practised joint search‑and‑rescue, illegal‑fishing interdiction, and oil‑spill response using shared MDA pictures.

Strategic coordination on the South China Sea is anchored in the 2016 Arbitral Tribunal award, which India has consistently supported in multilateral fora. The 2023 India‑Philippines Joint Statement on the SCS reaffirmed “freedom of navigation and overflight” and committed to regular consultations at the ASEAN‑India Summit and the East Asia Summit. A Track‑1.5 dialogue, the “Manila‑New Delhi Strategic Conversation”, convened in February 2024, bringing together think‑tank scholars and retired officials to draft confidence‑building measures (CBMs) for disputed features, including a proposed hotline between the Indian Navy’s Eastern Naval Command and the Philippine Navy’s Naval Forces West.

Institutional architecture comprises the Joint Defence Cooperation Committee (JDCC) at Defence Secretary level (last met in New Delhi, April 2024), the JPMC for BrahMos execution, and the WSA Technical Working Group for MDA data standards. The Philippines has also expressed interest in co‑producing the BrahMos‑NG variant, with a feasibility study launched in July 2024 under the JDCC’s technology‑transfer sub‑group.

India‑United Kingdom Defence and Technology Cooperation: Carrier Strike Group Interoperability, Jet Engine Development Dialogue, and Cyber Security Framework

The 2021 UK‑India Defence and International Security Partnership (DISP) laid the foundation for a multi‑domain defence relationship that now spans carrier strike group (CSG) interoperability, jet‑engine co‑development, and a dedicated cyber security framework. The Royal Navy’s Carrier Strike Group 21 (CSG‑21), led by HMS Queen Elizabeth, conducted its maiden Indo‑Pacific deployment in 2021, with a port call at Mumbai and a joint exercise “Konkan‑2021” focusing on anti‑submarine warfare (ASW) and air‑defence coordination. A follow‑up CSG‑23 deployment in 2023 included a bilateral “Exercise Konkan‑2023” off Goa, where Indian Navy’s P‑8I Poseidon and UK’s F‑35B Lightning II conducted joint targeting drills, validating the Link‑16 tactical data link interoperability.

Jet‑engine technology transfer is pursued through the Rolls‑Royce–HAL Strategic Partnership announced in July 2023. The MoU covers joint definition of a 110‑kN class turbofan for India’s Advanced Medium Combat Aircraft (AMCA), with Rolls‑Royce providing core‑engine design authority and HAL responsible for integration, testing, and indigenous production line setup at Bengaluru. A Joint Engine Development Board (JEDB), meeting quarterly, reviewed the preliminary design review (PDR) in March 2024 and cleared the critical design review (CDR) milestone for 2025. The UK Government’s Defence Exportability Assessment (DEA) granted an Open General Export Licence (OGEL) for the engine’s core technologies in December 2023, expediting technology flow.

Cyber security cooperation was institutionalised by the UK‑India Cyber Security Dialogue, inaugurated in London in November 2022. The dialogue established a Cyber Threat Intelligence Sharing Arrangement (CTISA) linking the UK’s National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) with India’s Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT‑In). The first operational exchange in May 2023 involved indicators of compromise (IoCs) related to the “Volt Typhoon” campaign targeting critical infrastructure in the Indo‑Pacific. A Joint Cyber Exercise “Cyber‑Shakti” is scheduled for 2025, simulating ransomware attacks on port management systems in Mumbai and Felixstowe.

Governance is provided by the Defence and International Security Partnership (DISP) Steering Committee at Ministerial level (last convened in London, September 2023), the Defence Equipment Sub‑Committee (DESC) at Secretary level, and the Cyber Security Working Group (CSWG) at agency level. The 2024 UK‑India Strategic Futures Forum, held in New Delhi, added a technology‑transfer track for quantum‑resistant cryptography, signalling expansion beyond traditional platforms.

India‑Germany Defence and Cyber Security Cooperation: Submarine Dialogue, AI‑Enabled Defence R&D, and Joint Working Group on Cyber Defence

Germany and India upgraded their defence ties with a Defence Cooperation Agreement (DCA) signed in Berlin on 14 June 2022, creating a structured dialogue architecture that includes a Submarine Dialogue, an AI‑Enabled Defence R&D Working Group, and a Joint Working Group on Cyber Defence (JWG‑CD). The Submarine Dialogue, launched in November 2022, addresses India’s Project‑75I requirement for six conventional submarines with Air‑Independent Propulsion (AIP). ThyssenKrupp Marine Systems (TKMS) submitted the Type 214 design in response to the Request for Information (RFI) issued in January 2023; a technical evaluation committee completed the Preliminary Technical Assessment in August 2023, shortlisting TKMS alongside Navantia (Spain) and Daewoo (South Korea) for the Request for Proposal (RFP) stage expected in 2025.

AI‑Enabled Defence R&D cooperation was formalised through a MoU between India’s Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO) and Germany’s Fraunhofer Institute for Communication, Information Processing and Ergonomics (FKIE) in March 2023. The MoU targets three pilot projects: (1) autonomous swarm‑drone coordination for border surveillance, (2) predictive maintenance algorithms for naval gas‑turbine engines, and (3) AI‑based electronic warfare (EW) signal classification. A joint DRDO‑Fraunhofer steering committee met in Hyderabad in January 2024, approving a €12 million seed fund for the swarm‑drone demonstrator, with flight trials slated for late 2025 at the Integrated Test Range, Chandipur.

The Joint Working Group on Cyber Defence (JWG‑CD), constituted under the DCA, held its inaugural session in New Delhi in October 2022. Its mandate covers critical‑infrastructure protection, secure 5G/6G network architecture, and joint incident‑response exercises. The first tabletop exercise “Cyber‑Shield‑2023” simulated a coordinated ransomware attack on the Indian Power Grid Corporation and the German Federal Office for Information Security (BSI) networks, resulting in a shared playbook for cross‑border attribution and mitigation. A follow‑up live‑fire exercise “Cyber‑Shield‑2024” is planned for June 2024, involving red‑team operators from both nations.

Strategic oversight is provided by the Indo‑German Defence Committee (IGDC) at Defence Secretary level (last met in Berlin, May 2024), the High‑Level Technology Dialogue (HLTD) at Ministerial level (annual, last in New Delhi, February 2024), and the Cyber Security Working Group at CERT‑In/BSI level (quarterly). The 2024 Indo‑German Defence Industry Forum in Munich showcased 15 joint ventures, including a Tata‑Rheinmetall armoured vehicle programme and a Mahindra‑MTU diesel‑engine line for future infantry combat vehicles.

  • Assuming that a single missile export (e.g., BrahMos to the Philippines) automatically upgrades the partnership to the highest strategic tier; the full suite of institutional mechanisms must be present.
  • Confusing a White Shipping Agreement with a reciprocal logistics support agreement; the former enables merchant‑vessel data exchange, the latter provides military refuelling, repair, and replenishment rights.
  • Overstating the operational readiness of co‑development projects such as the India‑Italy AW159 integration or the India‑Germany swarm‑drone demonstrator; most remain at feasibility or prototype stage as of 2024.
  • Treating a cultural exchange programme as a substitute for hard security alignment; soft‑power initiatives complement but do not replace defence dialogues.
  • Neglecting the connectivity bottleneck in India‑Thailand relations; the Myanmar segment of the Trilateral Highway remains a chokepoint where both nations depend on third‑party implementation.
  • Equating a renewable‑energy MoU with a binding investment treaty; financial closure and regulatory approvals are separate, often protracted processes.
  • Ignoring the role of sovereign wealth funds and development banks (e.g., EIB, KfW) as strategic vectors; their investment decisions can shape bilateral leverage beyond government‑to‑government agreements.

The advanced bilateral frameworks outlined above illustrate how institutional depth, multi‑domain engagement, and measurable deliverables differentiate strategic partnerships from transactional ties.

India‑United Kingdom Defence Logistics Agreement and Joint Maritime Exercises (2023‑2024)

Building on the 2023 Defence and International Security Partnership, India and the United Kingdom launched negotiations for a Reciprocal Access Agreement (RAA) that would grant each other’s armed forces streamlined logistical support, including refuelling, maintenance, and spare‑parts supply during port visits and joint operations. The RAA talks are conducted under a Joint Working Group on Logistics (JWG‑Log) that meets quarterly, with the first meeting held in New Delhi in March 2023 and the second in London in September 2023. Parallel to the RAA, a Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) dialogue was institutionalised in June 2023, linking the Indian Navy’s Information Fusion Centre‑Indian Ocean Region (IFC‑IOR) with the UK’s National Maritime Information Centre (NMIC). This dialogue enables real‑time sharing of white‑shipping data, satellite‑derived vessel tracks, and anomaly alerts across the Indian Ocean and the Western Pacific.

Operationalising the MDA framework, the two navies conducted the bilateral exercise “Konkan‑2023” in the Arabian Sea, featuring anti‑submarine warfare drills, coordinated boarding operations, and a joint air‑defence simulation involving Indian P‑8I Poseidon aircraft and UK Merlin helicopters. In early 2024, the UK Carrier Strike Group (CSG‑21), led by HMS Queen Elizabeth, made a port call at Visakhapatnam and participated in “Exercise Indra‑2024”, a tri‑service exercise that integrated the Indian Army’s mechanised infantry, the Indian Air Force’s Su‑30MKI fighters, and the Royal Navy’s F‑35B Lightning II jets. The exercise tested interoperable command‑and‑control procedures, cross‑deck helicopter landings, and joint logistics sustainment using the nascent RAA procedures.

A significant institutional milestone was the establishment of the India‑UK Defence Industry Forum (DI‑F) in November 2023, co‑chaired by the Defence Secretaries. The forum identified three priority co‑development tracks: (1) next‑generation anti‑ship missiles, (2) autonomous surface vessels for mine counter‑measures, and (3) cyber‑resilient communication suites for carrier groups. A memorandum of understanding between the Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO) and the UK Defence Science and Technology Laboratory (Dstl) was signed in February 2024 to advance the autonomous surface vessel prototype, with sea trials scheduled for late 2025 at the Indian Navy’s Karwar base.

Strategic oversight is provided by the Annual Defence Ministers’ Dialogue (last held in London, July 2024) and the Strategic Defence Review (SDR) Working Group, which aligns the RAA, MDA, and co‑development tracks with the broader Indo‑Pacific security architecture. The convergence of logistics reciprocity, shared maritime situational awareness, and joint industrial projects marks a shift from episodic exercises to a structured, multi‑domain defence partnership.

India‑Germany Green Hydrogen Partnership: Task Force, Pilot Projects, and Development Finance (2023‑2024)

The Indo‑German Green and Sustainable Development Partnership (GSDP), upgraded in 2022, created a dedicated Green Hydrogen Task Force (GHTF) that convened for the first time in Berlin in April 2023. The GHTF is co‑chaired by India’s Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE) and Germany’s Federal Ministry for Economic Affairs and Climate Action (BMWK). Its mandate covers technology transfer, standards harmonisation, and the design of bankable pilot projects that can be scaled to commercial volumes.

Two flagship pilots were approved in the GHTF’s second meeting (New Delhi, October 2023). The first, located at the Gujarat Industrial Development Corporation (GIDC) site in Dahej, involves a 100 MW electrolyser powered by dedicated solar‑wind hybrid capacity, with hydrogen earmarked for ammonia synthesis at a nearby fertiliser plant. The consortium includes GAIL (India), Siemens Energy (Germany), and the German development bank KfW, which committed a concessional loan of €150 million. The second pilot, in Tamil Nadu’s Thoothukudi district, targets a 50 MW electrolyser integrated with a desalination plant to produce green hydrogen for export‑oriented steel decarbonisation. This project leverages a €80 million equity investment from the European Investment Bank (EIB) and technical assistance from the Fraunhofer Institute for Solar Energy Systems (ISE).

Standards alignment is pursued through a joint working group on hydrogen purity, safety certification, and grid‑injection protocols, reporting to the International Partnership for Hydrogen and Fuel Cells in the Economy (IPHE). In March 2024, the group released a harmonised “Green Hydrogen Certification Framework” that mutually recognises Indian Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) and German DIN specifications, reducing duplication for cross‑border trade.

Financing architecture combines public‑sector concessional loans, multilateral grants, and private‑equity co‑investment. KfW’s “Green Hydrogen Facility” and EIB’s “Clean Energy Transition Fund” operate under a blended‑finance model that de‑risks early‑stage projects through first‑loss guarantees. The Indian side contributes viability‑gap funding (VGF) from the National Green Hydrogen Mission (NGHM), which allocated ₹19,700 crore for the 2023‑2025 period. A joint monitoring dashboard, hosted on the Indo‑German Energy Forum portal, tracks project milestones, electrolyser performance metrics, and carbon‑abatement outcomes in real time.

The partnership’s strategic significance extends beyond decarbonisation: it creates a technology corridor for electrolyser manufacturing, establishes a precedent for Indo‑European climate finance, and positions India as a credible green‑hydrogen supplier to European markets under the EU’s REPowerEU targets.

Examiner’s Technique: Comparative Mechanism Mapping
Compare the institutional architecture of the India‑UK Reciprocal Access Agreement negotiations with the India‑Germany Green Hydrogen Task Force. Identify two structural similarities and two functional differences.
Similarities: (1) Both operate through a dedicated joint working group (JWG‑Log for UK, GHTF for Germany) that meets at regular intervals and reports to a ministerial‑level steering body (Annual Defence Ministers’ Dialogue / Indo‑German Energy Forum). (2) Both embed a blended‑finance or cost‑sharing mechanism — UK logistics costs are offset through reciprocal access credits; Germany pilots use KfW/EIB concessional finance matched by Indian VGF. Differences: (1) The UK mechanism is primarily a security‑logistics framework aimed at operational interoperability, whereas the Germany mechanism is a climate‑technology deployment framework aimed at commercial scale‑up. (2) The UK architecture includes a parallel Maritime Domain Awareness dialogue linking operational centres (IFC‑IOR NMIC); the Germany architecture includes a standards‑harmonisation working group reporting to an international body (IPHE) rather than a direct operational data‑exchange link.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks you to “compare institutional mechanisms”, first list the formal bodies (working groups, steering committees), then note the financing or cost‑sharing model, and finally distinguish the primary policy domain (security vs climate). This three‑layer template — structure, finance, domain — works for any bilateral mechanism comparison.

India‑Japan Undersea Cable Resilience and Quad Connectivity Initiative (2023‑2024)

Recognising the vulnerability of submarine telecommunications cables to accidental damage and strategic disruption, India and Japan signed a Memorandum of Understanding on Submarine Cable Resilience in August 2023 under the aegis of the Information Fusion Centre‑Indian Ocean Region (IFC‑IOR). The MoU establishes a Joint Cable Monitoring Cell (JCMC) that fuses Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, satellite synthetic‑aperture radar (SAR) imagery, and cable‑owner telemetry to generate real‑time threat alerts for the critical Asia‑Europe and Asia‑America cable corridors traversing the Indian Ocean.

The JCMC conducted its first tabletop exercise, “Cable‑Shield‑2023”, in December 2023, simulating a coordinated severance of the SEA‑ME‑WE‑5 and the India‑Asia‑Europe (IAE) cables. Participants from the Indian Navy’s Directorate of Naval Intelligence, the Japan Coast Guard, and major cable consortiums (SubCom, NEC, and Tata Communications) produced a shared incident‑response playbook that defines escalation thresholds, repair‑vessel dispatch protocols, and diplomatic notification channels.

At the Quad Leaders’ Summit in May 2024, the four partners announced the Quad Undersea Cable Initiative (QUCI), a coordinated investment framework to diversify routing, enhance landing‑station redundancy, and develop a joint repair‑capacity pool. India pledged to host a regional cable‑repair depot at the upcoming deep‑water port in Vadhavan, Maharashtra, while Japan committed to supplying two purpose‑built cable‑repair vessels equipped with remotely operated vehicles (ROVs) by 2026. The United States and Australia contribute financing through the Development Finance Corporation (DFC) and the Australian Infrastructure Financing Facility for the Pacific (AIFFP), respectively.

Technical standards harmonisation is pursued via a Quad Working Group on Cable Specifications, which adopted the International Cable Protection Committee (ICPC) “Recommendation 12” for burial depth and armouring in high‑risk zones. The group also agreed on a mutual recognition arrangement for cable‑landing‑station security clearances, reducing administrative delays for cross‑border repairs.

Strategically, the initiative mitigates the “single‑point‑of‑failure” risk that could cripple financial transactions, government communications, and military command‑and‑control across the Indo‑Pacific. It also signals a collective commitment to preserving the open, rules‑based maritime order that underpins digital connectivity.

India‑Australia Cyber Security Dialogue and Critical Technology Track (2023‑2024)

The India‑Australia Cyber Security Dialogue, elevated to a ministerial‑level track in 2022, held its third session in Canberra in February 2023 and its fourth in New Delhi in August 2023. The dialogue is structured around three pillars: (1) threat‑intelligence sharing through a dedicated Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT‑In Australian Cyber Security Centre) secure channel, (2) joint capacity‑building for critical‑infrastructure operators, and (3) normative engagement in multilateral forums such as the UN Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) and the Paris Call for Trust and Security in Cyberspace.

In 2024, a Critical Technology Track (CTT) was added to the dialogue, focusing on quantum‑resistant cryptography, artificial‑intelligence safety, and 5G/6G equipment certification. A Joint Working Group on Quantum‑Resistant Cryptography (JWG‑QRC) was constituted in March 2024, co‑chaired by India’s Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C‑DAC) and Australia’s Defence Science and Technology Group (DSTG). The JWG‑QRC launched a collaborative research programme on lattice‑based key‑exchange protocols, with a target to produce a draft national implementation guideline by December 2025.

On AI safety, the two nations agreed to align their respective national AI ethics frameworks — India’s “Responsible AI for All” (RAI) and Australia’s “AI Ethics Principles” — through a bilateral mapping exercise completed in June 2024. The exercise identified convergent principles (transparency, accountability, human‑centric design) and divergent regulatory approaches (India’s sectoral guidelines vs Australia’s voluntary framework), informing a joint position paper for the G20 Digital Economy Working Group.

For 5G/6G equipment, a Mutual Recognition Arrangement (MRA) on security testing was signed in July 2024, allowing Indian telecom operators to accept Australian‑certified test results for base‑station hardware and vice‑versa. The MRA leverages the Common Criteria Evaluation and Certification Scheme (CCECS) and reduces duplicate testing costs for vendors such as Ericsson, Nokia, and home‑grown players like Tejas Networks and Australian firm Cohda Wireless.

Operationalising the dialogue, a joint “Cyber‑Shield‑2024” exercise was conducted in September 2024, simulating a ransomware attack on a shared energy‑grid SCADA system. The exercise validated the real‑time threat‑intelligence feed, coordinated incident‑response playbooks, and the legal‑framework for cross‑border data preservation under the Mutual Legal Assistance Treaty (MLAT).

India‑South Korea Defence Production and Autonomous Systems Co‑Development (2023‑2024)

Under the Special Strategic Partnership, the defence production pillar deepened with the expansion of the K9 Vajra‑T self‑propelled howitzer manufacturing line at the Larsen & Toubro (L&T) facility in Hazira, Gujarat. In March 2023, the Defence Acquisition Council (DAC) cleared an additional 100 units, raising the total order to 200. The production schedule incorporates a 30 % indigenous content target by 2025, achieved through joint development of the fire‑control system by DRDO’s Armament Research and Development Establishment (ARDE) and Hanwha Defense’s Korean counterpart.

Simultaneously, a Joint Development Programme (JDP) for Unmanned Ground Vehicles (UGVs) was launched in October 2023. The programme, governed by a bilateral Project Management Board (PMB) meeting biannually, aims to produce a modular 6×6 platform capable of logistics transport, reconnaissance, and casualty evacuation. The first prototype, designated “K‑UGV‑1”, completed mobility trials at the Indian Army’s Mahajan Field Firing Range in February 2024, demonstrating autonomous navigation in desert terrain using fused LiDAR‑camera perception stacks co‑developed by India’s Centre for Artificial Intelligence and Robotics (CAIR) and Korea’s Agency for Defense Development (ADD).

A Technology Transfer Agreement (TTA) for Active Protection Systems (APS) was signed in May 2024, covering the Korean “K‑APS” hard‑kill interceptor and the Indian “DRDO‑APS” soft‑kill electronic counter‑measure suite. The TTA stipulates joint integration testing on the Future Infantry Combat Vehicle (FICV) platform, with a combined live‑fire demonstration planned for the 2025 Defence Expo in Lucknow. Intellectual‑property rights are shared under a 50‑50 ownership model, with export‑control clauses aligned to the Wassenaar Arrangement.

Strategic oversight is provided by the Defence Ministers’ Dialogue (last held in Seoul, June 2024) and the Defence Industry Cooperation Committee (DICC), which monitors indigenisation metrics, supply‑chain resilience, and third‑country export potential. The partnership also explores co‑marketing of the K9 Vajra‑T and UGV platforms to Southeast Asian and Middle Eastern militaries, leveraging India’s “Make in India” ecosystem and Korea’s established defence‑export channels.

  • Assuming that a logistics agreement automatically grants full basing rights; the Reciprocal Access Agreement only covers refuelling, maintenance, and spare‑parts support during port visits.
  • Confusing the Green Hydrogen Task Force’s pilot‑project financing with a sovereign‑guaranteed off‑take contract; KfW and EIB loans are concessional but require commercial viability assessments.
  • Treating the Quad Undersea Cable Initiative as a binding treaty; it is a coordinated investment framework with voluntary contributions and no dispute‑settlement mechanism.
  • Equating the India‑Australia Mutual Recognition Arrangement on 5G testing with a full security‑certification equivalence; the MRA covers test‑result acceptance, not policy‑level security clearance.
  • Overstating the readiness of the K‑UGV‑1 prototype; as of 2024 it has completed mobility trials but not yet undergone operational evaluation in contested environments.
  • Neglecting the role of export‑control regimes (Wassenaar, MTCR) in the India‑South Korea APS technology transfer; compliance reviews can delay integration timelines.
  • Believing that a single joint exercise (e.g., Cyber‑Shield‑2024) establishes a permanent operational cyber‑defence alliance; sustained interoperability requires continuous threat‑intelligence sharing and legal‑framework updates.

India-China Trade Deficit Management: Strategic Economic Dialogue, Non-Tariff Barriers, and the Press Note 3 Investment Screening Regime

The bilateral trade deficit crossed USD 85 billion in 2023-24 (India imports ~USD 101 billion, exports ~USD 16 billion), the largest with any single country. The India-China Strategic Economic Dialogue (SED), institutionalised in 2011 under the NITI Aayog-Vice Chairman and China's National Development and Reform Commission (NDRC) Chairman, has met nine times (last in 2022 virtually). Its five working groups — Infrastructure, Energy, High Technology, Resource Conservation, and Policy Coordination — have produced zero binding market-access outcomes since 2018. The structural asymmetry stems from China's non-tariff barriers (NTBs): mandatory CCC certification for 400+ product categories (toys, electronics, auto parts), opaque drug registration by the NMPA (average 4-6 years vs 12-18 months in India), and standards divergence (GB standards vs IEC/ISO) that function as de facto import substitution tools. India's response operates on three tracks. First, Press Note 3 (2020) amended the FDI policy to mandate government approval for investments from countries sharing a land border — effectively China, Hong Kong, and Pakistan. As of March 2024, 380+ proposals were pending; clearance rates for brownfield pharmaceutical and auto-component investments improved to ~60% in 2023 after the DPIIT SOP (August 2023) introduced 60-day timelines and deemed-approval clauses for non-sensitive sectors. Second, Quality Control Orders (QCOs) under the BIS Act 2016: 67 QCOs notified since 2020 covering steel, chemicals, toys, electronics, and air conditioners — raising compliance costs for Chinese exporters. Third, PLI scheme design explicitly targets import substitution: the PLI for Advanced Chemistry Cells (ACC) (₹18,100 crore), Solar PV Modules (₹24,000 crore, Tranche II), and White Goods (₹6,238 crore) mandate domestic value addition thresholds (25-60%) that disqualify pure assembly of Chinese kits. The 2023-24 trade data shows early divergence: smartphone imports from China fell 22% YoY while exports to China (iron ore, cotton, shrimp, organic chemicals) grew 8%, but the deficit remains structural. The 14th Five-Year Plan (2021-25) Chinese "dual circulation" strategy prioritises self-reliance in semiconductors and high-end manufacturing, limiting market-access concessions. India's leverage lies in pharmaceutical API dependency (China supplies ~68% of India's API imports by value) and rare earth processing — both sectors where PLI and the Khanij Bidesh India Ltd (KABIL) joint venture (with three PSUs) seek alternative sourcing from Australia, Argentina, and Kazakhstan.

India-Russia Rupee-Ruble Settlement: Vostro Account Architecture, Surplus Rupee Utilisation, and 2023-2024 Sanctions Navigation

The Rupee-Ruble settlement mechanism, formalised through the RBI circular (July 2022) under the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), authorises Special Rupee Vostro Accounts (SRVAs) for Russian banks in Indian banks and vice versa. As of March 2024, 20 Russian banks (including Sberbank, VTB, Gazprombank, Rosselkhozbank) and 8 Indian banks (SBI, Canara Bank, UCO Bank, Indian Bank, Bank of Baroda, HDFC, ICICI, Axis) hold SRVAs. The mechanism operates on market-determined exchange rates referenced to the RBI's USD-INR rate and the Moscow Exchange RUB-USD rate, avoiding direct INR-RUB quoting. Trade settlement in 2023-24: ~USD 45 billion of the ~USD 65 billion bilateral trade (oil, coal, fertiliser, diamonds, defence) routed through SRVAs. The structural problem: India's imports (~USD 61 billion) vastly exceed exports (~USD 4 billion), leaving Russian banks with surplus rupee balances estimated at ₹40,000-50,000 crore (USD 5-6 billion) as of December 2023. These rupees cannot be repatriated (capital account non-convertibility) and face limited investment avenues: Indian G-Secs (FAR limit 6% for FPIs, but SRVA holders treated as non-residents), T-bills, and corporate bonds (within FPI limits). The RBI's August 2023 clarification permitted surplus rupees to fund Indian exports to Russia (trade credit) and investment in Indian infrastructure via NIIF, but uptake remains low due to sanctions risk. Sanctions navigation: The US Executive Order 14024 (April 2022) and EU 11th sanctions package (June 2023) target Russian financial institutions. Indian banks use non-SWIFT messaging (SFMS, SPFS) and currency swap lines (RBI-Bank of Russia ₹1.5 lakh crore / RUB 1.5 trillion, renewed 2023) to maintain plausible deniability. The S-400 delivery (completed December 2023) and RELOS (Reciprocal Exchange of Logistics Agreement) negotiations (stuck on jurisdictional immunity clauses) are settled outside SRVAs via rupee-denominated sovereign guarantees. The 2024 BRICS Kazan Summit endorsed BRICS Pay and a BRICS Cross-Border Payment Initiative (BCBPI) — Russia proposes linking SRVAs to a BRICS-ledger settlement layer using CBDCs (Digital Ruble pilot expanded to 12 banks in 2024; RBI's e₹-W wholesale pilot includes 13 banks). India remains cautious: the Finance Ministry's 2024 note flags AML/CFT compliance gaps in Russian KYC norms and the FATF grey-listing risk (Russia suspended from FATF in February 2023).

Examiner's Trap: Settlement Mechanism vs Currency Internationalisation
A 2024 Mains question asked: "The Rupee-Ruble Vostro mechanism demonstrates India's progress towards rupee internationalisation. Critically examine." Candidates often conflate bilateral settlement with internationalisation.
Correct framing: The SRVA mechanism is a bilateral barter-adjacent settlement, not internationalisation. Three distinctions: (1) Closed loop — rupees cannot be used for third-country payments or held by non-Russian non-residents; (2) No capital account convertibility — surplus rupees trapped in Indian assets with repatriation restrictions; (3) No offshore rupee market — NDF volumes for INR remain USD-linked (Singapore, Dubai, London), not RUB-linked. True internationalisation requires: (a) Asian Clearing Union (ACU) expansion to rupee invoicing (currently USD/ACU dollar); (b) RBI's 2022 INR invoicing circular uptake — only 18 countries approved, minimal volumes; (c) Offshore INR bond market (Masala bonds) revival — outstanding ~USD 4 billion (2024) vs USD 20 billion peak (2018). The SRVA is a sanctions workaround, not a currency regime change. Full marks require citing the RBI Annual Report 2023-24 (Chapter VI) which explicitly classifies SRVAs under "Bilateral Arrangements" not "Internationalisation Initiatives".
Exam-Day Strategy: Bilateral Settlement Mechanisms
When a question mentions "Vostro", "SRVA", "rupee trade", or "local currency settlement", immediately identify: (1) Which two central banks? (2) Trade asymmetry direction? (3) Surplus currency deployment options? (4) Sanctions context? (5) Distinction from multilateral frameworks (ACU, BRICS Pay, BIS mBridge). Draw a 2x2 matrix: Bilateral vs Multilateral × Current Account vs Capital Account. SRVA = Bilateral + Current Account only. This structure fetches 3-4 marks for "analytical framework" before content.

India-EU Trade and Technology Council: Three Working Groups, Regulatory Convergence on AI/Data/Green Tech, and the 2024 Ministerial Outcomes

The India-EU Trade and Technology Council (TTC), launched at the EU-India Summit (April 2022) and operationalised by the first Ministerial (Brussels, May 2023), is the second TTC for the EU (after US-EU TTC) and India's first. It operates through three Working Groups (WGs) co-chaired by MeitY (India) and DG CONNECT/DG ENER/DG TRADE (EU). WG1: Strategic Technologies, Digital Governance, and Digital Connectivity — delivered the 2023 Joint Statement on Trustworthy AI aligning India's DPDP Act 2023 principles (consent, purpose limitation, data minimisation) with the EU AI Act (2024) risk-based classification. A Joint Working Group on AI (established June 2024) will map high-risk AI use-cases (biometrics, critical infrastructure, employment) for mutual recognition of conformity assessments. On semiconductors, the 2023 MoU on Semiconductor Ecosystem enables joint R&D (C-DAC + imec/CEA-Leti) and talent mobility (EU Blue Card fast-track for Indian chip designers). WG2: Green Energy Technologies — produced the 2023 Green Hydrogen Dialogue outcomes: mutual recognition of certification schemes (India's Green Hydrogen Standard, 2023 vs EU's RFNBO Delegated Act), joint pilot projects (ACME-Ohmium 1 GW Gujarat + EU IPCEI Hy2Move funding), and EIB-India Hydrogen Bank co-financing (€500 million first tranche). Offshore wind: 2024 Joint Working Group finalised spatial planning alignment (India's 37 GW tender pipeline vs EU's 300 GW 2050 target) and supply-chain de-risking for rare-earth permanent magnets (India's KABIL + EU Critical Raw Materials Act strategic projects). WG3: Trade, Investment, and Resilient Supply Chains — the 2024 Ministerial (New Delhi, April 2024) advanced FTA negotiations (9th round, June 2024) on rules of origin (regional value content 40% for autos), geographical indications (350+ Indian GIs vs 3,500+ EU GIs), and public procurement (India's Make in India vs EU GPA commitments). The Critical Raw Materials Dialogue (separate from TTC, launched 2023) identified 14 joint projects (lithium brine in Rajasthan, graphite in Odisha, rare earths in Andhra). Data adequacy: The 2024 Data Adequacy Dialogue assesses India's DPDP Act 2023 against GDPR Article 45 — key gaps: government access exemptions (Section 7, 17), Data Protection Board independence, and cross-border transfer mechanism (whitelist vs adequacy). The EU's 2024 adequacy decision for Japan (12 years) sets the benchmark; India targets 2026 decision.

India-Gulf Strategic Petroleum Reserve Phase II: ADNOC/Aramco/KPC Participation, Commercial Terms, and Energy Security Calculus

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve (SPR) Phase II, approved by the Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (January 2021), adds 6.5 million tonnes (MT) across two sites: Chandikhol, Odisha (4 MT) and Padur, Karnataka (2.5 MT) — complementing Phase I's 5.33 MT (Visakhapatnam 1.33 MT, Mangaluru 1.5 MT, Padur 2.5 MT). The commercial model shifts from Phase I's government-funded fill to PPP-based "fill-for-share": foreign NOCs fill caverns at their cost, retain commercial ownership of crude, and pay storage fees (USD 0.5-0.7/barrel/month) to ISPRL (Indian Strategic Petroleum Reserves Ltd). In return, India gets first right of refusal (FRR) during supply disruptions at prevailing market price (not discounted). ADNOC (UAE) signed the first Phase II agreement (July 2023) for 1.5 MT at Mangaluru (Phase I cavern) — a pilot for the model. Saudi Aramco signed the Chandikhol Heads of Agreement (February 2024) for 2.5 MT (of 4 MT), with KPC (Kuwait) in advanced talks for the remaining 1.5 MT at Chandikhol + 2.5 MT at Padur. Commercial terms negotiated: (1) Crude quality — Arab Light (Aramco), Murban (ADNOC), Kuwait Export (KPC) — all compatible with Indian refinery slates; (2) Tenure — 10 years renewable, with mandatory 50% FRR trigger if global supply disruption >7 days (IEA collective action threshold); (3) Insurance/force majeure — ISPRL bears cavern integrity risk; NOCs bear crude quality/title risk; (4) Financing — NOCs can pledge crude stocks for working capital loans from Indian banks (SBI, EXIM) under RBI's ECB framework. Energy security calculus: Phase I+II = 11.83 MT ≈ 9.5 days of net imports (2023-24: 4.6 mbpd). The IEA 90-day net import cover norm requires ~90 MT — India's total commercial + strategic stocks ~75 MT (including OMC tanks). The Gulf NOC participation serves three strategic purposes: (a) Lock-in supply relationships — Aramco/ADNOC/KPC already supply ~45% of India's crude; (b) Diplomatic signalling — UAE/SA/Kuwait get equity stake in India's energy security architecture; (c) Price discovery — Murban futures (ADX, launched 2021) and Dubai Mercantile Exchange (DME) Oman crude benchmark gain relevance for Indian term contracts. The 2024 ISPRL board decision to explore Phase III (additional 12 MT) at Rajkot (Gujarat) and Chandikhol expansion targets 2030 operationalisation with potential US SPR swap arrangements (India-US Strategic Clean Energy Partnership pillar).

India-Chabahar Port 10-Year Agreement (2024): Operational Terms, INSTC Eastern Corridor Integration, and Afghanistan/Taliban Engagement Protocols

The 10-year long-term agreement for Chabahar Port operations was signed on 13 May 2024 between India Ports Global Ltd (IPGL) and Port and Maritime Organization (PMO), Iran, replacing the 2016 interim agreement (renewed annually). Key operational terms: (1) Investment commitment — USD 120 million equipment procurement (6 mobile harbour cranes, 12 RTGs, 2 reach stackers, fire-fighting systems) + USD 250 million for Phase 1B development (berths 6-10, 14m draft, 800m quay) over 10 years; (2) Revenue share — IPGL pays USD 22.5 million annual fixed fee (escalated 3% YoY) + variable royalty (8% of gross revenue) to PMO; (3) Cargo guarantee — India commits 2.5 MT/year (wheat, urea, steel, machinery) ramping to 8 MT/year by Year 5; (4) Sanctions carve-out — US State Department specific licence (SL-2024-05) issued May 2024 authorises IPGL transactions, dollar payments via UCO Bank/IDBI Bank (non-US correspondent), and humanitarian cargo exemptions (wheat to Afghanistan). INSTC Eastern Corridor integration: Chabahar is the maritime gateway for the International North-South Transport Corridor (INSTC) Eastern Branch — Chabahar → Zahedan → Zaranj (Afghanistan) → Chabahar-Zahedan railway (628 km, IRCON/RAILTEL executing, 40% complete as of March 2024, target December 2025). The 2023 INSTC Coordination Council (Moscow) ratified the Chabahar-Zahedan-Mashhad-Sarakhs-Turkmenistan rail link as the primary Eastern Corridor, reducing Mumbai-Moscow transit from 40 days (Suez) to 18-22 days. Afghanistan/Taliban engagement protocols: Since August 2021, India has shipped 50,000 MT wheat + 2,000 MT pulses + 40,000 MT urea via Chabahar to Afghanistan (UN WFP/FAO implemented). The 2024 agreement includes a dedicated "Afghanistan Humanitarian Corridor" clause: (a) Priority berthing for Afghan-bound cargo; (b) Waived storage charges (first 30 days); (c) Taliban customs coordination — India engages de facto Taliban Ministry of Commerce via Qatar-mediated channel for transit permits (no formal recognition). The Chabahar-Zahedan railway connects to Zaranj-Delaram highway (India-built, 2009) enabling door-to-door delivery to Kabul/Herat. Geopolitical hedge: The agreement coincides with Iran's 25-year China strategic partnership (2021) — China committed USD 2.7 billion for Chabahar Phase 2 (petrochemical zone) but zero disbursement as of 2024 due to US sanctions. India's operational control of Shahid Beheshti terminal (berths 1-5) prevents Chinese monopoly. The 2024 BRICS expansion (Iran member) and SCO connectivity agenda provide multilateral cover for Chabahar's development outside Western financial architecture.

  • Conflating bilateral settlement mechanisms with currency internationalisation — SRVA, ACU, and BRICS Pay are distinct; only multilateral current+capital account frameworks qualify as internationalisation steps.
  • Assuming Press Note 3 blocks all Chinese investment — it mandates government approval; 60%+ clearance rate in non-sensitive sectors (pharma brownfield, auto components) post-2023 SOP.
  • Treating TTC Working Group outputs as binding agreements — WG deliverables are joint statements, roadmaps, and pilot projects; regulatory convergence (AI Act vs DPDP, GDPR adequacy) requires separate legislative/executive action.
  • Overstating SPR Phase II fill volumes — agreements are Heads of Agreement/MoUs; physical crude fill for Aramco/KPC not commenced as of mid-2024; only ADNOC's 1.5 MT at Mangaluru (Phase I) is operational.
  • Equating Chabahar 10-year agreement with full port ownership — IPGL operates Shahid Beheshti terminal (5 berths) only; PMO retains sovereignty, land ownership, and control over Phase 2 petrochemical zone (China-linked).
  • Ignoring the surplus rupee trap in Rupee-Ruble mechanism — ₹40,000-50,000 crore stuck in SRVAs with no viable deployment; G-Sec investment limited by FAR, infrastructure investment blocked by sanctions risk.
  • Confusing INSTC Eastern Corridor (Chabahar-based) with Western Corridor (Azerbaijan-Russia) — Eastern Corridor serves Afghanistan/Central Asia; Western Corridor serves Russia/Europe; different rail gauges, different geopolitics.

India's Engagement with Pacific Island Countries: Forum for India‑Pacific Islands Cooperation (FIPIC) — Climate, Maritime, and Development Partnership

The Forum for India‑Pacific Islands Cooperation (FIPIC) was launched during Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s visit to Fiji in November 2014, marking India’s first structured outreach to the 14 Pacific Island Countries (PICs): Cook Islands, Fiji, Kiribati, Marshall Islands, Federated States of Micronesia, Nauru, Niue, Palau, Papua New Guinea, Samoa, Solomon Islands, Tonga, Tuvalu, and Vanuatu. The inaugural summit in Suva (19‑20 November 2014) established a leaders‑level mechanism that meets every two to three years; the second summit was held in Jaipur (21‑22 August 2015) and the third in Port Moresby, Papua New Guinea (22 May 2023).

The partnership rests on three pillars. First, climate resilience and sustainable development: India pledged USD 1 million annually to the Pacific Islands Forum Secretariat’s climate change fund, financed solar‑powered community projects in Fiji, Kiribati, and Tuvalu through the International Solar Alliance, and committed lines of credit (LoCs) worth USD 150 million for infrastructure, renewable energy, and water security. The 2023 summit announced a dedicated Pacific Islands Climate Resilient Infrastructure Facility with an initial corpus of USD 50 million. Second, maritime domain awareness and blue economy: India deployed the INS Satlaya for hydrographic surveys in PIC waters, shared satellite‑based vessel tracking data via the Information Fusion Centre – Indian Ocean Region (IFC‑IOR), and supported capacity building in maritime law enforcement through the Indian Coast Guard’s training programmes. Third, humanitarian assistance and capacity building: during the COVID‑19 pandemic, India supplied over 1.2 million vaccine doses to PICs under Vaccine Maitri; the Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation (ITEC) programme reserves 200 annual slots for PIC nationals in agriculture, IT, and public administration.

Strategically, FIPIC amplifies India’s Indo‑Pacific narrative by securing diplomatic support in multilateral fora — PICs collectively hold 14 votes in the UN General Assembly and are pivotal in seabed mining negotiations at the International Seabed Authority. The 2023 Port Moresby summit coincided with the India‑Papua New Guinea Defence Cooperation Agreement, enabling Indian Navy port calls and joint exercises. However, implementation gaps persist: LoC disbursement rates remain below 40 per cent due to limited absorption capacity, and China’s Belt and Road investments (e.g., the Nadarivatu hydropower project in Fiji) continue to outpace Indian commitments. The 2024‑25 budget allocated INR 300 crore for FIPIC‑specific projects, signalling a push to convert pledges into deliverables before the fourth summit scheduled for 2025 in New Delhi.

India's Engagement with the Caribbean Community (CARICOM): Historical Ties, Development Cooperation, and Multilateral Alignment

India’s relationship with the 15‑member Caribbean Community (CARICOM) is anchored in a shared history of indentured labour migration (1838‑1917) that created a vibrant Indian diaspora exceeding 1.5 million across Guyana, Trinidad & Tobago, Suriname, and Jamaica. The first India‑CARICOM Foreign Ministers’ Meeting convened in New York on 25 September 2019 on the margins of UNGA74; the second virtual meeting on 18 February 2021 institutionalised a biennial dialogue. A Joint Commission mechanism, established in 2005, oversees sectoral cooperation in agriculture, health, ICT, and renewable energy.

Development partnership operates through concessional LoCs and grant assistance. Since 2015, India has extended LoCs totalling USD 210 million: USD 30 million for a 10 MW solar plant in Guyana (commissioned 2022), USD 15 million for the CARICOM Regional ICT Centre in Barbados (operational 2021), and USD 50 million for the Pan‑Caribbean Health Surveillance Network (launched 2023). Under Vaccine Maitri, India donated 500,000 Covishield doses to nine CARICOM states between February and May 2021, followed by commercial supplies of 1.2 million doses. The 2023 India‑CARICOM Business Conclave in Georgetown identified pharmaceuticals, fintech, and agro‑processing as priority trade sectors; bilateral merchandise trade, however, remains modest at USD 1.8 billion (2023‑24), heavily skewed toward Indian exports of refined petroleum and pharmaceuticals.

Multilateral convergence is the partnership’s strongest strand. India and CARICOM coordinate closely in the UN, Commonwealth, WTO, and the Alliance of Small Island States (AOSIS). India supported CARICOM’s “Loss and Damage” agenda at COP27 and COP28, pledging USD 10 million to the Santiago Network for technical assistance. In return, CARICOM members backed India’s candidature for the UN Security Council non‑permanent seat (2021‑22) and the International Solar Alliance’s observer status at the UN. The 2024 India‑CARICOM Summit (held virtually on 15 April) adopted a Plan of Action 2024‑2028 focusing on digital public infrastructure transfer (UPI‑style payment rails), climate‑smart agriculture, and diaspora engagement through the Pravasi Bharatiya Divas framework. Structural constraints — geographic distance, small market size, and limited air connectivity — continue to impede deeper economic integration, prompting exploration of a virtual trade corridor leveraging the India‑Stack architecture.

Comparative Analysis Technique
Question: "Compare and contrast India's development partnership models with the Pacific Island Countries (FIPIC) and the Caribbean Community (CARICOM). Highlight the strategic drivers and implementation challenges unique to each." (15 marks, 250 words)
Answer: India's engagement with FIPIC and CARICOM shares the template of South‑South cooperation — grant‑cum‑LoC financing, capacity building via ITEC, and diaspora leverage — but diverges in strategic calculus and delivery architecture. Strategic drivers: FIPIC is driven by Indo‑Pacific maritime security, blue‑economy access, and UN vote consolidation in a region where China’s BRI presence is expanding rapidly; CARICOM engagement is rooted in historical diaspora ties, multilateral alignment on climate justice (AOSIS coordination), and pharmaceutical market access. Institutional architecture: FIPIC operates through a leaders‑level summit (three held since 2014) with a dedicated Pacific Division in MEA since 2020; CARICOM relies on a Foreign Ministers’ dialogue and a Joint Commission (last met 2021) without a standalone secretariat. Financial instruments: FIPIC LoCs (USD 150 million) prioritise climate‑resilient infrastructure and maritime domain awareness assets; CARICOM LoCs (USD 210 million) focus on solar energy, regional ICT, and health surveillance networks. Implementation challenges: FIPIC suffers from low absorptive capacity (disbursement <40%), geographic dispersion across vast EEZs, and competition from Chinese concessional financing; CARICOM faces small market fragmentation, limited air/sea connectivity, and regulatory heterogeneity across 15 sovereigns. Innovation: FIPIC pioneers satellite‑based MDA sharing via IFC‑IOR; CARICOM pilots digital public infrastructure transfer (UPI stack) as a virtual trade corridor. Both partnerships illustrate India’s differentiated neighbourhood‑plus diplomacy: FIPIC secures strategic depth in the Indo‑Pacific, CARICOM amplifies Global South solidarity in climate negotiations.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for comparison across two regional groupings, structure the answer in a four‑column mental table: Strategic Driver, Institutional Mechanism, Financial Instrument, Implementation Bottleneck. Fill each column with one specific data point (summit year, LoC amount, disbursement rate, competing power). This fetches both analytical depth and factual precision within the word limit.

India's Nuclear Diplomacy: Civil Nuclear Agreements, NSG Membership Bid, and Non‑Proliferation Credentials

India’s civil nuclear diplomacy transformed after the 2008 Nuclear Suppliers Group (NSG) waiver, which exempted India from the NPT‑linked full‑scope safeguards requirement. Since then, India has concluded 14 civil nuclear cooperation agreements: United States (10 October 2008), France (30 September 2008), Russia (11 December 2009), Canada (28 June 2010), Kazakhstan (16 April 2009), Mongolia (15 September 2009), Argentina (28 October 2010), Namibia (25 September 2009), United Kingdom (7 February 2010), South Korea (25 January 2011), Czech Republic (6 February 2013), Australia (5 September 2014), Japan (11 November 2016), and Sri Lanka (23 February 2015 — limited to training and research). The agreements enable uranium supply (Kazakhstan, Canada, Australia, Niger), reactor technology transfer (Russia’s Kudankulam VVER‑1000 units 3‑6, France’s Jaitapur EPRs — under negotiation), and joint R&D (India‑France Centre for Advanced Research on Nuclear Energy, established 2019).

India’s NSG membership bid, formally submitted in May 2016, remains stalled primarily due to China’s insistence on a “criteria‑based approach” that links membership to NPT accession. The 2016 Seoul plenary and subsequent meetings (Berne 2017, Jurmala 2018, Nur‑Sultan 2019, virtual 2020‑21, Vienna 2022‑23) saw no consensus. India’s non‑proliferation credentials rest on: (1) a voluntary unilateral moratorium on nuclear testing since 1998; (2) adherence to NSG and Missile Technology Control Regime (MTCR) guidelines since 2008 and 2016 respectively; (3) membership in the Wassenaar Arrangement (December 2017) and Australia Group (January 2018); (4) a robust export control system (SCOMET list) aligned with multilateral regimes; and (5) active participation in Fissile Material Cut‑off Treaty (FMCT) negotiations at the Conference on Disarmament. The Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage Act, 2010 (CLNDA) remains a friction point — supplier liability concerns have delayed Jaitapur and Kovvada projects; the 2023 India‑France Strategic Dialogue established a joint working group to explore an insurance‑pool mechanism compliant with CLNDA and the Convention on Supplementary Compensation (CSC), which India ratified in 2016.

Strategically, nuclear diplomacy serves energy security (target: 22.48 GW nuclear capacity by 2031‑32), technology acquisition (fast breeder reactors, SMRs), and normative influence — India positions itself as a responsible nuclear power outside the NPT, advocating universal, non‑discriminatory disarmament. The 2024 IAEA General Conference saw India pledge USD 1 million to the IAEA’s Nuclear Security Fund and announce a regional training centre for nuclear security in partnership with the Global Centre for Nuclear Energy Partnership (GCNEP), Bahadurgarh.

India's Space Diplomacy: Bilateral Cooperation with France, Russia, Japan, and the EU beyond NISAR

While the NASA‑ISRO NISAR mission (2023 Artemis Accords signing, 2024 launch) dominates headlines, India’s space diplomacy spans a dense network of bilateral mechanisms. France: The CNES‑ISRO partnership, formalised in 1993, has delivered Megha‑Tropiques (2011, tropical climate monitoring), SARAL‑AltiKa (2013, oceanography), and the upcoming TRISHNA mission (2025‑26, high‑resolution thermal infrared for water stress). France provides the Viking‑2B engine for the Gaganyaan service module and hosts Indian astronaut candidates at the European Astronaut Centre, Cologne. The 2023 India‑France Space Dialogue added a Maritime Domain Awareness constellation using AIS payloads on French satellites. Russia: Glavkosmos‑ISRO cooperation under the 2018 agreement covers Gaganyaan astronaut training (four Indian Air Force test pilots completed training at Yuri Gagarin Cosmonaut Training Centre, 2020‑21), supply of semi‑cryogenic engine technology, and navigation satellite compatibility (GLONASS‑NavIC interoperability). The 2024 Moscow summit reaffirmed joint lunar exploration under the International Lunar Research Station (ILRS) framework. Japan: JAXA‑ISRO collaboration focuses on the Lunar Polar Exploration (LUPEX) mission (target 2026‑27, Japanese rover on Indian lander), X‑ray astronomy (XRISM, launched 2023 with Indian contribution), and space situational awareness (SSA) data sharing via the 2023 SSA MoU. The 2024 Tokyo summit established a joint working group on satellite‑based 5G/6G backhaul. European Union: The 2023 EU‑India Space Dialogue operationalised Copernicus data access for Indian agencies (free, full, open), initiated Galileo‑NavIC signal‑in‑space compatibility testing, and included India in Horizon Europe calls for space weather and debris mitigation research. Commercial dimension: NewSpace India Limited (NSIL) has secured launch contracts for OneWeb (36 satellites, 2022‑23), and is negotiating with European operators for LVM3 slots. The 2024 Indian Space Policy explicitly encourages foreign direct investment up to 74 per cent in satellite manufacturing and 49 per cent in launch vehicles, aiming to position India as a competitive launch and manufacturing hub.

India's Cyber Diplomacy: Bilateral Cyber Dialogues, Capacity Building, and Norm‑Shaping in Multilateral Fora

India’s cyber diplomacy architecture comprises structured bilateral dialogues, multilateral norm‑building, and developmental capacity building. Bilateral dialogues: India‑US Cyber Relationship Framework (signed 2016, upgraded 2022 with a Joint Working Group on Critical Infrastructure Protection); India‑UK Cyber Dialogue (annual since 2018, 5th edition London 2023 focused on ransomware and 5G security); India‑France Cyber Dialogue (2019, 2022 Paris — joint statement on supply‑chain security); India‑Japan Cyber Dialogue (2020, 2023 Tokyo — cooperation on IoT security standards); India‑EU Cyber Dialogue (2020, 2023 Brussels — alignment on EU Cyber Resilience Act and India’s Digital Personal Data Protection Act); India‑Australia Cyber Dialogue (2021, 2024 Canberra — critical mineral supply‑chain cyber resilience); India‑Singapore Cyber Dialogue (2022, 2023 — fintech security and cross‑border data flows). Each dialogue produces a joint action plan with measurable deliverables: CERT‑In exercises, joint threat‑intel sharing, and academic exchanges.

Multilateral norm‑shaping: India participates actively in the UN Group of Governmental Experts (GGE) and the Open‑Ended Working Group (OEWG) on ICT security. India’s core positions: (a) existing international law, including the UN Charter, applies to State conduct in cyberspace; (b) sovereignty and non‑intervention are paramount — no “right to hack back”; (c) capacity building must precede prescriptive norms for developing countries. At the 2023 OEWG substantive session, India co‑sponsored a resolution on “Capacity Building for Global Cyber Stability” with 42 co‑sponsors. India hosted the Global Conference on Cyberspace (GCCS) in 2017 (New Delhi, 5,000+ delegates) and chaired the 2024 Global Forum on Cyber Expertise (GFCE) regional meeting for South Asia.

Capacity building: The ITEC programme offers 15 specialised cyber courses annually (malware analysis, digital forensics, SCADA security) to 300+ officials from partner countries. CERT‑In operates the “Cyber Surakshit Bharat” initiative extended to ASEAN, BIMSTEC, and African Union partners via virtual labs. The 2024‑25 budget allocated INR 120 crore for international cyber capacity building, including a proposed India‑Africa Cyber Fellowship. Challenges include attribution ambiguity in state‑sponsored attacks (e.g., 2022 AIIMS ransomware, 2023 power‑grid probing), data localisation demands conflicting with cross‑border data flow commitments, and the absence of a bilateral mutual legal assistance treaty (MLAT) in cybercrime with several key partners.

  • Conflating FIPIC and CARICOM as identical “Small Island Developing States” groupings — FIPIC members are Pacific‑focused with large EEZs and seabed mining stakes; CARICOM members are Caribbean‑focused with historical diaspora links and AOSIS climate leadership.
  • Assuming the 2008 NSG waiver equals NSG membership — the waiver permits nuclear trade; membership requires consensus on NPT‑linked criteria, which China blocks.
  • Treating all civil nuclear agreements as reactor‑supply deals — many (e.g., Kazakhstan, Namibia, Mongolia) are uranium‑supply or training‑only agreements.
  • Equating NISAR with the entirety of India‑US space cooperation — Artemis Accords, SSA, and commercial launch services are separate tracks.
  • Believing bilateral cyber dialogues produce binding treaties — they yield joint statements, action plans, and CERT‑level exercises, not legally enforceable instruments.
  • Overstating India’s role in UN cyber norm‑setting — India shapes narratives but has not led a GGE report; the 2021 GGE consensus report was chaired by Singapore.
  • Ignoring the CLNDA‑CSC interface in nuclear project delays — supplier liability concerns are resolved through insurance pools, not legislative amendment, a nuance often missed in answer writing.

India‑Bhutan Hydropower Project Financing and the 2023‑2024 Tariff Dispute

Bhutan’s hydropower portfolio is the cornerstone of its economic partnership with India. The 720 MW Mangdechhu plant, commissioned in 2019, was financed through a 70 % loan from the Export‑Import Bank of India at a fixed 10 % interest rate and a 30 % grant component from the Government of India. The 1,200 MW Punatsangchhu‑I project, still under construction, follows a 60 % loan / 40 % grant split, while the 1,020 MW Punatsangchhu‑II and the 600 MW Kholongchhu projects adopt a 50 % loan / 50 % grant structure. All loan agreements are denominated in Indian rupees, shielding Bhutan from exchange‑rate risk but linking debt service to India’s monetary policy.

In 2023 Bhutan formally requested a tariff revision for Mangdechhu and Punatsangchhu‑I, citing cost overruns of 12‑15 % caused by geological surprises and the COVID‑19 construction slowdown. The Joint Technical Committee (JTC) met in Thimphu in September 2023 and again in New Delhi in February 2024. The 2024 outcome, ratified by both cabinets, approved a 5 % upward revision of the sale price per unit for Mangdechhu (from ₹ 4.12 to ₹ 4.33/kWh) and a 4 % increase for Punatsangchhu‑I (from ₹ 4.05 to ₹ 4.21/kWh). The revised tariffs are indexed to the Indian Wholesale Price Index (WPI) for the next ten years, providing a built‑in inflation hedge.

The tariff adjustment has direct implications for Bhutan’s debt sustainability. The International Monetary Fund’s 2024 Article IV consultation noted that the higher revenue stream reduces the debt‑to‑GDP ratio from a projected 115 % to 108 % by 2027, keeping it within the IMF’s “moderate risk” threshold. However, the revision also raises the cost of electricity for Indian distribution utilities in Assam and West Bengal, prompting the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC) to initiate a parallel review of cross‑border power purchase agreements (PPAs) in March 2024.
