Strategically, the dispute underscores the evolving nature of India‑Bhutan energy diplomacy: from a grant‑heavy model in the 2000s to a loan‑dominant framework that now incorporates market‑linked tariffs. The 2024 agreement also includes a clause for joint monitoring of sediment management in the Mangdechhu reservoir, addressing a long‑standing environmental concern raised by Bhutan’s National Environment Commission.

India‑Nepal Mahakali Treaty Implementation and the 2023 Joint Committee Outcomes

The Mahakali Treaty, signed in February 1996 and ratified in 1997, governs the shared utilisation of the Mahakali (Kali) River. Its centrepiece is the Pancheshwar Multipurpose Project (PMP), envisaged at 4,800 MW with a 315 m high dam, and the Sarada Barrage for irrigation. The treaty establishes a Joint Committee (JC) that meets annually to review water‑release schedules, flood‑forecasting data exchange, and project progress.

The 2023 JC meeting, held in Kathmandu on 12‑13 June, produced three concrete deliverables. First, both sides agreed on a revised dry‑season release schedule: India will release a minimum of 150 m³/s from the Tanakpur barrage between November and May, up from the previous 120 m³/s, to support Nepal’s downstream irrigation and drinking‑water needs. Second, a real‑time flood‑forecasting protocol was operationalised, linking India’s Central Water Commission (CWC) hydro‑meteorological stations with Nepal’s Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DHM) via a dedicated satellite link; the first test run in August 2023 successfully transmitted peak‑flow data for the 2023 monsoon. Third, the JC endorsed the updated Detailed Project Report (DPR) for Pancheshwar, prepared by the joint venture of NHPC (India) and NEA (Nepal). India committed to financing 50 % of the DPR cost (approximately ₹ 250 crore) and to fast‑track environmental clearance through the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC).

Implementation hurdles remain. Land acquisition for the reservoir area in Nepal’s Darchula district has stalled due to local opposition over compensation rates; the 2024 Nepalese budget allocated ₹ 30 crore for resettlement, but the final package awaits parliamentary approval. On the Indian side, the Uttarakhand state government has raised concerns about seismic risk, prompting the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) to commission an independent seismic hazard assessment, expected by late 2024.

The treaty’s dispute‑resolution mechanism — a three‑member arbitral tribunal — has never been invoked. The 2023 JC minutes explicitly reaffirmed the preference for bilateral technical resolution, a stance that aligns with the 2021 “Neighbourhood First” policy directive emphasising confidence‑building over legalism.

Technique: Comparative Timeline Analysis
Compare the financing structures of the Mangdechhu and Punatsangchhu‑I projects and explain how the 2023 tariff revision affects Bhutan’s debt sustainability.
Step 1 – Identify financing ratios: Mangdechhu – 70 % loan (EXIM Bank, 10 % fixed) / 30 % grant. Punatsangchhu‑I – 60 % loan / 40 % grant. Step 2 – Note tariff change: 2023 JC approved 5 % increase for Mangdechhu (₹ 4.12 → ₹ 4.33/kWh) and 4 % for Punatsangchhu‑I (₹ 4.05 → ₹ 4.21/kWh), indexed to WPI. Step 3 – Link to debt sustainability: Higher per‑unit revenue raises annual cash flow by roughly ₹ 120 crore for Mangdechhu and ₹ 95 crore for Punatsangchhu‑I. IMF 2024 Article IV projects debt‑to‑GDP falling from 115 % to 108 % by 2027, moving Bhutan into the “moderate risk” band. Step 4 – Mention counter‑effect: Indian utilities face higher purchase costs, triggering CERC review of cross‑border PPAs (March 2024). Conclusion: The revision improves Bhutan’s fiscal trajectory but introduces a new negotiation layer with Indian regulators.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “financing structure + tariff impact”, tabulate the loan‑grant split, interest rate, currency denomination, and the latest tariff percentage change. Then quote the most recent IMF or World Bank debt‑sustainability assessment. This two‑column method (financial terms → macro impact) scores full marks for analytical depth.

India‑Bangladesh Railway Connectivity: The Akhaura‑Agartala Line and Trade Facilitation

The 15.06 km Akhaura‑Agartala cross‑border railway line, inaugurated on 1 November 2023 by the Prime Ministers of both countries, is the first broad‑gauge link connecting India’s Northeast with Bangladesh’s rail network. The project, funded entirely as a grant by the Government of India at an estimated cost of ₹ 1,000 crore, was executed by the Northeast Frontier Railway (NFR) in coordination with Bangladesh Railway. The line runs from Akhaura (Bangladesh) to Agartala (Tripura) and includes a 1.5 km bridge over the Gumti River.

Operational trials began in January 2024; commercial freight services commenced on 15 March 2024. The first freight rake carried 2,500 tonnes of cement from Bangladesh to Tripura, reducing transit time from 48 hours (road) to 6 hours (rail). Passenger services, slated for the second half of 2024, will cut the Agartala‑Kolkata journey from 38 hours to 24 hours via the new corridor.

Trade facilitation measures accompany the rail link. The 2023‑24 Joint Working Group (JWG) on Customs harmonised the Electronic Data Interchange (EDI) protocol, enabling pre‑arrival filing of manifests. A dedicated “Green Channel” at Akhaura customs reduces clearance time for perishable goods (e.g., fish, horticulture) from 12 hours to under 3 hours. The Bangladesh‑India Friendship Power Company (BIFPCL) has also signed a memorandum to supply 500 MW of electricity from the upcoming 1,320 MW Maitree Super Thermal Power Plant to Tripura via the new rail‑linked transmission corridor.

Strategically, the line operationalises the 2015 “Protocol on Inland Water Transit and Trade” (PIWTT) by providing a multimodal alternative to the congested Petrapole‑Benapole road crossing. The 2024 World Bank Logistics Performance Index (LPI) notes a 0.3‑point improvement in Bangladesh’s “timeliness” sub‑index, attributed partly to the rail link. For India, the corridor strengthens the “Act East” policy by giving the landlocked Northeast a direct, low‑cost export gateway to the Bay of Bengal.

India‑Sri Lanka Energy Cooperation: LNG Terminal Development and Power Grid Interconnection

Energy security has become a new pillar of India‑Sri Lanka bilateral engagement after Sri Lanka’s 2022 sovereign debt crisis. In June 2023, Indian Oil Corporation (IOC) and Ceylon Petroleum Corporation (CPC) signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) for a 5 mtpa LNG regasification terminal at Kerawalapitiya, near Colombo. The terminal, to be built on a build‑own‑operate (BOO) basis, will receive LNG from IOC’s long‑term contracts with Qatar and the United States. The project cost is estimated at US $1.2 billion, with IOC holding a 51 % equity stake and CPC 49 %. Financial closure is targeted for Q4 2024, and commercial operations are expected by 2026.

Parallel to the LNG terminal, a high‑voltage direct current (HVDC) interconnection between Madurai (Tamil Nadu) and Anuradhapura (North Central Province) is under feasibility study. The 2023 MoU between Power Grid Corporation of India (PGCIL) and the Ceylon Electricity Board (CEB) envisages a 500 MW bipole link, with a submarine cable crossing the Palk Strait. The joint technical committee completed the marine route survey in February 2024 and submitted the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) to Sri Lanka’s Central Environmental Authority (CEA) in May 2024. India has pledged a concessional line of credit of US $400 million for the Indian side of the converter station.

The interconnection addresses two strategic objectives. First, it allows Sri Lanka to import cheaper base‑load power from India’s southern grid, reducing its reliance on expensive diesel generation (which accounted for 38 % of generation in 2022). Second, it creates a platform for future renewable‑energy trade; India’s 2024 National Green Hydrogen Mission identifies the Madurai‑Anuradhapura corridor as a potential route for exporting green‑hydrogen‑derived electricity.

Implementation risks include Sri Lanka’s domestic regulatory approvals — the Public Utilities Commission of Sri Lanka (PUCSL) must approve the power purchase agreement (PPA) tariff — and the geopolitical sensitivity of submarine cable routing near the contested Katchatheevu waters. Both sides have agreed to a “confidence‑building” clause in the MoU that any dispute will be referred to a bilateral technical panel before escalation to arbitration.

  • Assuming the 2023 tariff revision for Bhutanese hydropower automatically resolves all debt‑sustainability concerns without referencing IMF projections.
  • Conflating the Mahakali Treaty’s Pancheshwar project with the earlier Tanakpur barrage; they are distinct infrastructure components with separate financing.
  • Treating the Akhaura‑Agartala line as a purely passenger project; freight revenue and customs harmonisation are the primary economic drivers in the first two years.
  • Overstating the LNG terminal’s immediate impact — commercial operations begin only in 2026, and the 2023 MoU is a framework, not a final investment decision.
  • Ignoring the HVDC interconnection’s dependence on Sri Lanka’s PUCSL tariff approval; the technical feasibility does not guarantee commercial operation.
  • Equating the 2023‑24 Joint Committee minutes with a legally binding amendment of the Mahakali Treaty; they are operational directives, not treaty modifications.
  • Believing the “Green Channel” at Akhaura applies to all cargo categories; it is limited to perishable and high‑value goods as per the 2023 customs notification.

The advanced sections above illustrate how financing details, joint‑committee outcomes, and multimodal connectivity clauses shape the strategic calculus of India’s neighbourhood diplomacy.
